Chapter 17


Chapter Summary

“I love your Patranus, Harry. I mean, Patronus. That was not a Freudian slip.”

Or…

harry: *actual, reasonable observations on how the patronus works*

voldemort: *shoujo anime opening*



Chapter Notes

You know those stories where Harry is slowly seduced to the Dark side and discovers his hidden affinity for Dark magic? Well, this is the exact opposite—Voldemort slowly being seduced into discovering that he may be capable of Light magic after all. Not enough to be on the “Light side,” certainly, but enough to be on Harry’s side.

While Voldemort will never abandon his passion for Dark magic, he has it in him to cast powerful Light spells like the Patronus, which is a game-changing event for him both character-wise and in terms of Harry’s view of him. It’ll be so much harder (heh) for Harry to tell himself he isn’t attracted to Voldemort when he can see and feel Voldemort’s ability to love manifested physically through the Patronus.

It will now be clear to Harry that this isn’t the Voldemort Harry had left behind in his original timeline… and that the reason for that change is Harry himself.

Flattering, huh? I mean, someone changing who they are for you, to be a better person for you? Now that’s love. Dangerously codependent love, perhaps, but love nonetheless.

lmao here in tomarrymort land are we not all about dangerous codependency??



See the end of the chapter for more notes



Midnight. The moon was at its zenith above the overgrown backyard that Voldemort had not yet ordered the house-elf to organise; he had plans to convert it into a botanical garden for potion ingredients, and did not trust the elf to have as careful a hand with the laying of the magical topsoil as he himself had. He would’ve called upon Abraxas, given how devoted a Herbologist he was, but Voldemort couldn’t rely on ingredients that may have been tampered with by a saboteur. Though to expect sabotage of Abraxas was to vastly overestimate his intelligence. Morgane, however… It was a compliment to her that she was worthy of Voldemort’s caution.

Harry had insisted that before they began the lesson, Voldemort read the several dusty and neglected tomes he had on the history and symbology of Patronuses, only to become resentful when Voldemort managed it all in under three hours using comprehension and memory enhancement charms of his own invention—the real reason behind his success as a Hogwarts student. So much for Harry’s charmingly Gryffindor delaying tactics.

“This is so unfair,” Harry groused. “You just skim books and get everything inside them while the rest of us pathetic mortals have to slog through miles of reading.”

“Ah, so you’re finally admitting I am a god?”

Harry knocked his shoulder against Voldemort’s and pointed sternly at the porch facing the backyard. “Cast.”

Voldemort walked to the edge of the porch and fingered his wand. He felt no Light magic within him, nothing that would make him capable of casting a Patronus charm. Perhaps he could only channel the Light through Harry, with the bond between them acting as a conduit. But no, he had to do this himself. A Patronus would only present itself when invoked by a wizard’s own natural magic; no borrowing or stealing of someone else’s magic would make it possible, or he’d have been capable of a Patronus a long time ago, by siphoning Light magic out of his enemies.

He tilted his head, running through the seemingly endless parade of memories he had of Harry, despite only having met him recently. “Do you ever struggle with choosing a memory for the Patronus spell?”

“What?”

“It is astounding that in two days, I have gone from having no memories befitting of the spell, to such a surplus that I am struggling to choose only one. It is astounding…” Voldemort met Harry’s eyes. “And humbling.”

Harry swallowed. “Yeah, I get that first part. I struggled with not having a good memory, too, when I learned the Patronus.”

The now ever-present hatred Voldemort harboured for his future self flared. That he’d done that to Harry. That he’d destroyed Harry’s childhood and had taken the Patronus away, however temporarily, from such an extraordinarily gifted Light wizard. “And when was that?”

Harry shuffled his feet. “When I was thirteen,” he mumbled.

Voldemort’s jaw dropped. “Thirteen?” And yet again, Harry had proved himself to be more than a mere genius, as Tom Riddle had been. Harry was beyond genius, beyond even the total mastery of magic. Harry was the embodiment of magic itself.

Harry shuffled some more and then steeled himself, adopting the unbiased persona of a professor. “Just… think of all of your positive memories at once, then, if you have too many. I know the books said to focus on one, but what actually works is having one coherent emotion behind your Patronus; it’s just that for most people, it’s easier focusing on one memory to distill it to its single most dominant emotion. That’s how it works for me, too. But everybody’s different. You could be different. Just give it a go and see what works for you.”

“Reasonable advice, Harry. Perhaps I should focus on one person, rather than one memory.”

Harry huffed, embarrassed. “Enough of your flattery, now! Cast.”

Voldemort inhaled deeply. He shut his eyes and focused inwards, on that place within him that had been alight when Harry had merged with him, when Harry had touched him—and been touched by him—more deeply than either of them had ever experienced. Voldemort recalled what Harry’s Light had felt like in his magical core, in his soul, and how it had not seemed unnatural or strange. Light magic had not been unsuited to him, unlike what he had always feared. Perhaps it had always been there, just too buried and steeped in Darkness to truly be given the chance to manifest.

Had it always been there? In his present meditative state, Voldemort could discern, in hindsight, the flame-like flicker in his heart every time he had seen Harry smile or laugh or stand under the snow. Voldemort relived every instance of his life since Harry had swept into it, and found that there had been something bright within him from the very moment that he had initially encountered Harry, though he had not recognised it then: a sleeping ember of Light fanned to life by Harry’s fearlessness, Harry’s gentleness, Harry’s kindness.

Nobody else’s kindness had ever changed Voldemort so. To him, all those who had been kind to him had simply been weak. But Harry was so demonstrably not weak—so undeniably, flabbergastingly powerful—that Voldemort could not mistake Harry’s kindness for anything but what it was. It was an act of immense courage against the cruelty of the human race, a courage that Voldemort himself had heretofore lacked, heretofore misunderstood. It was as though Harry’s Light magic had looked into Voldemort, found its hidden counterpart, and summoned it to the surface. A siren’s call. Irresistible.

Ergo, latent and insubstantial though it was, Voldemort was capable of Light magic. It was there in him, and enough of it to recognise its mate in Harry’s. It was there in him. It was there. He only had to learn how to summon it from himself like Harry did.

The most sensible approach would be to use what had worked before. So Voldemort relived the smoothness of Harry’s hair under his palm at the ball; he relived the shape of Harry’s body when he’d held Harry close; he relived the silkiness of Harry’s skin in Tabitha’s changing room. He relived the quirk of Harry’s mouth when Harry was taunting him about treacle tarts at the Malfoys’ dining table; he relived the rage flashing like lightning in Harry’s eyes that evening before the party, with Harry atop him on the couch; he relived the sensation of Harry’s fingers twining with his in the bookshop; he relived the exquisite rapture of merging with Harry there, in that little nook that had been the entire universe to them then.

And as if on cue, a wave of brightness welled up within Voldemort like a tide drawn up by the moon and pulled towards the shore. He wasn’t in control of it, but perhaps that was the point, after a lifetime of seeking control over everything including death. The chief allure of Dark magic was control, after all, or the illusion of control. But this was the opposite: an inexorable inflorescence of Light, more akin to a blooming than a conjuring, more organic than forced. It welled up and up, a surge gaining momentum the longer it went uninterrupted by his terror or his ego, the longer he refused to suppress it as he had been doing subconsciously since childhood. He had believed that suppressing this ‘weakness’ in him was what had kept him alive, was what had made him a survivor. Instead, it had all but killed him. It had restricted him. It had robbed him of the very thing he had always secretly wanted—absolute power of every sort, Light and Dark.

Now, he opened his soul up to the Light as he never had before, and that burgeoning surge became an eruption of such heat and luminosity that he briefly thought he had been blinded by it. But he had not been blinded; he’d had his blindness burned out of him.

Indeed, when he opened his eyes, his very vision felt fundamentally altered, as if he was looking upon a world remade. And in the centre of that remade world, up there in the sky, was an immense Thestral.

Voldemort stared.

“Oh,” whispered Harry from beside him, stunned. “So you’re definitely not scared of death anymore.”

Yes, that was no longer a fear of Voldemort’s, let alone his greatest fear. Losing Harry was. Voldemort wondered, fleetingly, if a Boggart would reflect that change. He had no inclination towards finding out.

Instead he could only keep staring at his Patronus, so large and lambent that it was as though all the Light that had been trapped inside Voldemort for decades had sought desperate release in its form. Catharsis.

The Thestral was as black as the night itself, but with a marrow of starlight within it, its bones luminous and translucent against the backdrop of a great abyss. It was even more beautiful than Dark magic was on its own, for it was illuminated, light pulsing within its dark, transparent flesh. A perfect merging of his own Darkness and Harry’s Light—a finely wrought twining of shimmering silver and inky, formless ether.

In the Stygian depths of Voldemort’s soul flickered to life a hope so foreign to him that he did not immediately recognise it for what it was. Instead, a delicate wire of pain slid into him, like a pick into a lock, cracking him open. It was subtle, it was sweet, and it was agonising. It was a glimpse of a reality that he had long presumed he was exiled from, forever—a glimpse of his own Light magic, not encountered by proxy through Harry, but directly through himself—not a dazzling blaze like Harry’s, admittedly, but a quiet lustre upon his skin and within his mind, the blossoming of a type of magic in him that he had always considered himself incapable of.

This, this was what Harry was to him. Endless discovery, endless potential, endless bliss. Voldemort was becoming more than he had ever been, and not through struggle, as he was accustomed to, but through ecstasy. This was Voldemort’s first encounter with the Light within himself.

A profound gratitude took root in him. His fell to his knees slowly, gazing up at his Patronus as a man waking from a sleep of a thousand years would gaze upon the sun. It wasn’t until Harry sank down beside him that Voldemort realised that he had taken hold of Harry’s hand, and that Harry had knelt with him.

The Thestral gazed back at Voldemort, a seeming recognition in its glimmering eyes. Then it kicked up its hooves and galloped around them twice before making off towards the sky. Harry and Voldemort watched its ascent until it faded into the stars.

Voldemort tightened his grasp on Harry’s hand and looked at him. “Thank you,” Voldemort said, his voice thick, his eyelashes wet—an alien, unfamiliar sensation that he blinked away.

In the moonlight, Harry’s eyelashes were damp, too. He gripped Voldemort’s hand just as hard. “No,” he murmured, hushed. “That was all you. You made that happen.” He smiled, a small, true, tremulous smile, and in his wide, awestruck eyes was a mirror of Voldemort’s own hope—the hope that Voldemort was even more than he had ever allowed himself to be. “Well done.”

Those two simple words, so uncomplicated, so softly spoken, shook Voldemort. He had been praised aplenty by professors and followers alike, but this unadorned praise from Harry somehow meant more than any fawning, eloquent flattery he had ever received. He lifted Harry’s hand to his mouth and brushed a kiss over Harry’s knuckles.

Harry sucked in a breath.

“You have a talent, my dear,” Voldemort said, “for not taking credit where it is very much due. You are why I was capable of this. Now, put me out of my misery—as you also have a talent for doing—and show me what your Patronus is.”

“You’ll be disappointed,” Harry warned, though with palpable self-doubt.

“We’ll see,” Voldemort responded serenely.

Harry rolled his eyes.

Voldemort stepped back to give Harry room to cast, noting that Harry swished his wand up and across in a pattern not mentioned in any of the books Voldemort had just read. How like Harry, to make everything unique to himself, to brand it as his own, just as he had branded Voldemort’s soul.

Harry was clearly more than acquainted with casting this spell, for he cast it with authority. Voldemort frowned in momentary jealousy of those that had previously fuelled the spell for Harry. But that jealousy dwindled when Harry’s eyes cut quickly to him, mid-pronunciation, followed by a colouring of Harry’s cheeks in what, despite the moon’s achromatising dimness, could only be a blush.

It was just as clear that Harry’s current fuel for the Patronus was Voldemort.

Voldemort must have smirked, because Harry huffed at him again in mild irritation just a split second before his Patronus emerged.

It began as a swirl of magic that only took form after a hesitant pause—the whirlpool of amorphous Light moving one way, then the other. Perhaps it signified a struggle between what Harry’s Patronus had been and its current form. Voldemort tried not to view this as a test of loyalty, of affection. He would treasure his son even if that son’s Patronus did not match his own.

Nevertheless, Voldemort held his breath as hooves formed, and then a tail that wasn’t much more than an undecided wisp. That these were the cardinal features to coalesce was telling—perhaps Harry’s erstwhile Patronus had shared them—but following these more generic physical traits were far rarer skeletal limbs, aglow even more brilliantly with Light than Voldemort’s Patronus, and a pair of bony, leathery wings as diaphanous as smoke.

At the sight of them, a thunderous hurricane of joy rattled through Voldemort.

Another Thestral. Voldemort was not surprised. Elated, overwhelmed, relieved… but not surprised.

Harry, however, was. His expression was an odd mixture of shock and resignation, and the noise he let out was half-gasp, half-sob. Voldemort drew near him again and wrapped a comforting arm around Harry, who stiffened before sagging in defeat.

Defeat was not what Voldemort was experiencing, nor what he wanted Harry to experience. He wished for Harry to exult in the same singing triumph that was reverberating through Voldemort’s brain.

But Voldemort did not say what he was thinking, for it would be tactless to be victorious before Harry’s grief over the loss of his old Patronus. Voldemort did not say that what had occurred was inevitable. For how could soulmates have Patronuses that differed from each other? Whether either or both of their Patronuses were male or female was as yet unknown, and irrelevant; whether it was Harry’s Thestral or Voldemort’s that was the mare was of no consequence. Either way, they were a matched pair. Fated.

Harry’s Thestral was visibly younger than Voldemort’s, and more nimble and spirited, besides. Whereas Voldemort’s Thestral had possessed a certain gravitas, a certain majesty, Harry’s was innocent and playful. It tossed its mane mischievously, bent its head to snort right in Voldemort’s face—a puff of frosty, Light-scented air—and cantered off into the clouds with a distinctly untameable wildness. The very wildness that Harry had.

Beautiful. It was beautiful. And perfect, like Harry.

Harry, who now had a fist wound tightly in Voldemort’s robes, as if to keep himself upright.

“Harry,” Voldemort said carefully. “Are you—”

“If you ask me whether I’m all right, I’ll call my Patronus right back to snort at you some more.” Harry squeezed his eyes shut, his lashes even wetter than they had been before. “My Patronus is obviously really bloody happy.” He sounded somewhat betrayed by it.

“But you’re not,” Voldemort inferred. With difficulty, he asked, “Who... Who was... Who did your last Patronus represent?”

Harry choked on another half-sob. “My mum. The one you—the one the other you—”

“The one my other self murdered,” Voldemort concluded, teeth grit against his now-familiar fury. Was there any end to Voldemort’s reasons for hating his future self? But now, oh, now he was looking after Harry, not that charlatan from the future. Never again would Voldemort visit such sorrow upon his heir. “I am sorry,” he said, with such atypical sincerity that Harry peered at him through the dark.

“Wow.” Harry seemed stumped. “You mean it.”

“The loss of the Patronus that symbolised your mother must be terribly wounding to you. Terribly saddening. Like reliving her death.”

Harry tensed. “Don’t describe too many of my emotions, okay? I’m busy pretending they don’t exist.”

“Harry.” Voldemort couldn’t hide his concern.

“Tom.” Harry uttered Voldemort’s boyhood name in a tone that could have been mocking, had it not been heavy with… something. Regret, but also wonder. And, perhaps, fondness. “I’ll be fine. She’ll always be a part of me.”

Voldemort manfully stamped out the envy attempting to grow in him. “I see.”

Harry laughed. It was a shaky laugh, hoarse with unshed tears, but still a laugh. “You’re such a child sometimes. Are you sure you’re the parent here?” Harry released Voldemort’s robes only to gently grasp Voldemort’s wrist, raising Voldemort’s hand until it was cupping Harry’s cheek.

Voldemort shuddered at the invitation. At the license Harry had just given him, to touch Harry more. To take even more from Harry than he had already taken—a Patronus; a cause; an identity; a timeline. He ghosted his fingers along Harry’s jaw, Harry’s throat.

“I’m yours, too, you know,” Harry stated quietly, unsteadily. “It’s not like you didn’t just see the evidence of that with your own eyes.”

“You saw it, as well.”

“Yeah, yeah, we both saw it. The ultimate proof.” Harry leaned his head against Voldemort’s chest and sighed. “Time we got to work, then, I guess. Enough mutual emoting. You wanna hit up the Blacks, tomorrow? Drop in for a bit of a chat and ask them vaguely or not so vaguely about the contents of their vault, which you as their Lord should have an accounting of?”

Voldemort blinked. “An astonishingly Slytherin strategy given that you have not yet ceased weeping.”

Harry sniffled rebelliously. “Hey, I learned from the best.”

Indignantly, Voldemort began, “I do not weep—”

“Not on the outside, anyway. And it’s hilarious how you automatically assumed you’re the best.”

“Am I not?” Voldemort rested his chin atop Harry’s soft, soft hair. He meant the question in jest, since it was Harry who was the best, but Harry countered him in complete seriousness.

“You’re amazing,” Harry declared with rock-solid sincerity, calling up a rush of uncharacteristic warmth to Voldemort’s cheeks. “You literally just cast your first Light spell despite being a Dark Lord, and it was the Patronus, of all things. The one spell many people—including Dumbledore, I think—see as a test of goodness.”

“Goodness is an archaic and useless concept.” Voldemort seethed as usual at any mention of Dumbledore. “My feelings for you are wholly self-serving, I assure you.”

“And mine for you?” Harry asked, as if he expected an answer.

Voldemort had none. So Harry was admitting—what was Harry admitting? That he had feelings for Voldemort, too, that equalled or at least rivalled Voldemort’s feelings for him? That couldn’t be. Harry could not possibly crave him as much as he craved Harry, and for once, Voldemort did not resent that. “You,” Voldemort said roughly, hungrily, “are goodness itself.” He found himself amending his former position; goodness was not useless, not when it was Harry’s. “The term applies to nobody but you.”

Harry laughed again. “I guarantee you that there are better people than me out there, Tom.”

“Not to me,” Voldemort tightened his hold on his precious son. “Not to me.”

Voldemort woke at five in the morning with one overruling preoccupation: Could Thestral Patronuses be seen by others?

He mentally flipped back through the books Harry had commandeered him into reading yesterday, but no, hardly any mentioned Patronuses that were magical creatures, let alone those that could be invisible. Thestrals could not be seen except by those who had witnessed death; were Thestral Patronuses the same? If so, they could prove to be valuable secret messengers or spies if he or Harry were ever separated, or if they were seeking a means to communicate in public, amidst the general Wizarding populace, amidst the children at Hogwarts who by and large had not seen death, or amongst Light-oriented wizards and witches who had not been in the thick of the war.

Hm. An interesting tactical consideration. He would have to put it to the test post-haste.

A second consideration that drew Voldemort’s attention was from Catullus Spangle’s prescient book, Charms of Defence and Deterrence, in which Spangle wrote, “the Patronus is the awakened secret self that lies dormant until needed, but which must now be brought to light…” Or, in Voldemort’s case, be brought to Light. Fascinating. It implied what Voldemort already suspected, that his Light magic had been dormant but very much present for all these years, a part of his “secret self,” a self that Harry had awakened. Voldemort would meditate on it when he next summoned his Patronus.

He was also intrigued by the possibility of a Dark equivalent to the Patronus. What if a wizard’s wrath or hatred could be shaped into its own corporeal manifestation, a negative mirror image to the traditional, Light-fuelled Patronus? Voldemort had often felt that his magic had taken on Obscurus- or Lethifold-like qualities when he was enraged, and though he had thought the similarity more metaphorical than actual, he now wondered whether his Dark magic might actually take either of those forms, were he to conjure it corporeally. A Dark Patronus had never, to Voldemort’s knowledge, been attempted in Wizarding lore, but he was confident he could manage it if only he were to visualise Mulciber’s hateful, leering, dull-witted face.

Voldemort scowled as he shrugged into his gown. Perhaps he should avoid thinking of Mulciber so early in the morning; it would ruin his day. So he would think, instead, on what he would call his new Dark spell, his new Dark Patronus. A Sicarius, maybe, after the Latin for ‘assassin’. It would be not a protective, defensive projection of the caster’s magic, but an aggressive, offensive one. Just as the Patronus was intended to protect, to be the caster’s ‘patron’, a Sicarius would be intended to destroy, to be the caster’s ‘assassin’. Voldemort shivered at the prospect of Harry casting a Sicarius, and lost himself, for a few minutes, in the shape of the Basilisk that he’d seen Harry’s magic take on in Garrick’s shop. Oh. Oh, yes.

What would it take to draw Dark magic out of Harry, who seemed so incapable of it? He’d displayed an admirable viciousness when defending his godfather—that damned pretender—and could thus perhaps be convinced to dip his lovely, radiant, unblemished soul into the darkness, like the nib of a white quill dipped in ink. A tease more than a completion, for Voldemort did not delude himself that Harry would ever be truly Dark, or even marginally Dark.

But… but Voldemort could indulge in his fantasies, could he not? Just a little, by imagining the flaring of Harry’s honest rage… It would not burn cold, like Voldemort’s, but searingly hot, a hurricane of Fiendfyre, all-consuming, all-devouring. It would not even need to be Dark to be deadly, to be devastating. Light magic was just as destructive if left unchecked.

Even as he was, Harry was already fully capable of annihilating his enemies. He would be incandescent, feral, more fang and claw than fang and venom. A glorious ripping and rending, the beast of Light that Harry had controlled with such finesse and delicacy finally being let loose upon a crowd of Death Eaters—influential witches and wizards reduced to sticky crimson smears, throats torn out, gutted where they stood. Voldemort pictured it. He couldn’t stop picturing it.

Harry, laying waste. Harry, obliterating. Harry, conquering.

Harry, splattered with blood, flushed with exertion and gleaming with perspiration, his eyes blown black and savage.

Voldemort shifted restlessly, the material of his gown against his skin seeming somehow more present, more tangible. More like a touch than a fabric. Bemused, he adjusted his gown’s belt as he swept downstairs and into the kitchen.

No, he did not need violence from Harry to see Harry as beautiful. He cherished Harry’s principles and Harry’s unbending conscience, and knew that Harry would never, ever lose control of the immense beast within him, not even upon provocation, because that was the extent of Harry’s power. It was not merely magical power, but power over himself, exercised daily and with more discipline than Voldemort had ever exercised over anything, despite having considered himself, in his youth, among the most disciplined human beings in history.

Once again, the sheer magnitude of Harry’s power made him shiver, for to see it unleashed would be a spectacle, but to see it contain itself against all odds would be a display of strength unparalleled in all the world.

The kitchen was hotter than usual when Voldemort entered it; he’d have to order Flopsy to ease off on the warming charms. Strange, that everything from his gown to the ambient temperature of his home seemed ill-fitting, today. Perhaps it was simply the magnitude of his internal transformation yesternight, upon summoning his own Patronus; he would undoubtedly take some time to become acclimatised to being a Dark Lord and a Light wizard. His body and his mind would both have to make adjustments.

Upon seeing Harry at the stove, as expected, Voldemort slunk up behind him and kissed him on the nape. If Voldemort parted his lips ever-so-slightly to taste the saltiness of Harry’s sweat, well, surely he could not be blamed. It wasn’t as though Harry would notice.

Still, Harry jerked and swore, nearly knocking over the boiling pot of tea. “S-sodding hell!” He turned around and shoved Voldemort lightly in the shoulder, only to snatch his hand back when it slipped on the silk of Voldemort’s half-open gown and landed on Voldemort’s bare chest. “Step back a bit, would you, Tom? Or you’ll scald us both. And what on earth is happening with your face?”

“What?” Voldemort checked his features; he was far less aware of what they were doing around Harry than he was in the company of others, perhaps because he was less guarded with his son.

“You look like—” Harry reddened and gulped. “Never mind, you’re probably just. Um. Starving. For food! I’ll call Flopsy, since if I dare break into the pantry I’m pretty sure she’s padlocked by now, she’ll tie me to a chair until I let her cook us an entire stuffed pheasant for breakfast.”

Voldemort withdrew, the image of Harry’s wrists bound to a chair—or bound at all—refusing to leave his psyche. “Of course,” he said blankly, and sat without being quite aware of it.

“No reaction to a stuffed pheasant?” Harry joked, pouring them their teas and setting the cups on the table. “I’d have figured you’d love that sort of extravagant Pureblood brekkie.”

The term brekkie was so ridiculous that it snapped Voldemort out of his haze. “From what I’ve heard, Harry, the young Lucius Malfoy breakfasts on nothing more extravagant than Belgian waffles drizzled in milk chocolate and topped with fresh Wexford strawberries.”

Harry’s stomach growled. “Yeah,” he muttered irritably, “like that’s not extravagant. I used to be lucky if I got an orange rind for breakfast. Or lunch. Or dinner. Or all three put together.”

“At your Muggle guardians’?” Voldemort asked casually, as if he wasn’t planning to murder them all, and not in their sleeps, either.

Harry squinted at him. “Don’t think I can’t spot your murderous impulses just because you’re hiding them behind that weird, ‘I’m a completely normal person, no really’ smile.”

Pity. It worked on everyone else. “So you will not tell me their names.”

“Nope,” Harry popped the ‘p’. “Not even if you bribe me with real smiles.”

Voldemort raised an eyebrow. “My smiles are bribe-worthy?”

Harry fiddled with the handle of his teacup. He’d gone pink all the way to his nose, which was adorable. “Yeah, erm. I mean. Only the real ones. Like the ones you had yesterday—”

“After the Patronus, or before?”

“You mean after we traumatised Flopsy, or before?” Suddenly, Harry sat up. “Speaking of Flopsy, where is she? We didn’t, I dunno, drive her into a much-deserved retirement, did we?”

“Perhaps she fears showing herself because she understands that were it not for you, I would have very much liked to punish her for interrupting our conversation last night.”

“Conversation,” Harry repeated, just as blankly as Voldemort had spoken earlier. “You think she’s traumatised by you potentially dismembering her and not... not anything else?”

Voldemort sipped his own tea, luxuriating in its simplicity and sweetness, so like Harry’s. “What else?”

Harry gawked at Voldemort for nigh on a whole minute before groaning and thumping his head on the table. “Holy crickety crock,” Harry said, which made no sense, followed by a low, miserable, “Flopsy, are you there? I’m sorry, okay? Flopsy?”

There was a quiet pop. “Master Harry,” said Flopsy from behind Harry, causing him to jerk and swear once more. Flopsy stood as straight as her lumpy, arthritis-riddled limbs allowed her, and looked directly at the wall across the room, studiously not meeting Harry’s eyes.

Harry wilted.

Multiple platters laden—overladen, even—with hash browns, rashers of bacon, toasts, and maple syrup appeared on the table in front of Harry. And for the first time ever, Flopsy crossed over to Voldemort’s side to personally lay out his dishes, by hand.

Mystified, Voldemort watched her conduct this inexplicable ceremony, apparently just for him.

When Voldemort’s plate was set with a neatly folded napkin, fork and knife, Flopsy pronounced, in a voice so ringingly clear that it paralleled Morgane Malfoy’s in intimidation, “If Master Lord Voldemort will be touching Master Harry when Master Harry is not wanting it, sir, I will be reminding Master Lord Voldemort that Flopsy is a hundred-and-ninety years old and knows where all the poisons are hidden.”

And it turned out that Flopsy hadn’t met Harry’s eyes not because he discomfited her, but because she did not wish to discomfit him, for the stare she levelled at Voldemort was so afire with maternal malice that it was vaguely surprising Voldemort didn’t spontaneously combust.

Then Flopsy smiled, a wrinkled, toothless, flawlessly servile smile, and Voldemort reflected in a state of utter bewilderment that perhaps house-elves could be Sorted into Slytherin, too.

Without further ado, Flopsy vanished with her trademark pop.

“Is your fearlessness contagious?” Voldemort asked Harry dazedly. “Because I am fairly certain my house-elf just threatened to assassinate me at my own dining table.”

Harry did not respond.

Voldemort narrowed his eyes at Harry, who had collapsed sideways onto the table, his head buried in his arms, his shoulders shaking. “Harry?”

Harry flapped a hand as though he was dying. He emitted a wheezing sound. Flopsy wouldn’t have poisoned Harry, would she, given that Harry was her fragile golden child that she was protecting from a terrible tyrant who, what, should not even touch his own heir?

Voldemort rose, growing worried. “Harry—”

“I just,” Harry wheezed like an out-of-tune accordion, “I just—I’ve never been more humiliated in my life, but that was also simultaneously the most badass thing I’ve ever seen, and I’d pay good money to see it again.” And then Harry burst into unrestrained giggles, punctuated by yet more wheezing, as tears of laughter streamed down his cheeks.

Voldemort sat back down and sighed. He couldn’t very well punish Flopsy for making Harry laugh, which was always a desirable outcome. He cast a detection spell on his food, as he would now be doing for the foreseeable future, and proceeded to eat the hash browns when they were deemed free of all known poisons. While he was immune to many, there was no point in taking chances. “‘Badass’ is not a proper word, Harry.”

Harry wiped the tears from his eyes. “Not in your vocabulary, it isn’t.”

“Okay, so let’s go over our plan.” Harry bounced on his feet nervously as Voldemort added the finishing touches to Harry’s outfit; they were in Harry’s bedroom again, in front of the mirror. “We get access to the Black vault once you tell Mrs Black you’ll sell me to her for several heads of cattle like some medieval bride with a dowry—”

“Heads of house-elves, more likely,” Voldemort murmured, “given how she collects them like trophies.”

Harry grimaced. “Don’t remind me.”

“You’ve been to Grimmauld Place, then? Is that where you met her portrait, in the future?”

“Like I said. Don’t remind me.” Then Harry added with delightful vindictiveness, “Wonder how she’d feel getting her head chopped off and mounted on a fucking wall.”

“No beheading my followers, Harry.” Except that he rather wished Harry would. Preferably while letting Voldemort watch.

“Why would I even have to?” Harry rolled his eyes. “That’s your hobby.”

Voldemort straightened the cufflinks on Harry’s sleeves, because Harry’s idea of buttoning his shirt-cuffs was to leave the cufflinks off-centre and tilted haphazardly in different directions, when the cufflinks should, instead, always reflect each other precisely. Perfectly aligned, just like his and Harry’s souls. “Do not underrate your value. Any Pureblood match worth his or her name is worth a sizeable dowry, often of precious Dark artefacts and other highly prized items, along with a Gringotts vault or two. If I offered you to her daughters for free, it would be highly suspect. You are worth more than Walburga’s entire fortune, and she knows it.”

Harry snorted. “The chance to wed a daughter of hers to the Dark Lord’s heir? Yeah. Priceless.”

“No, Harry.” Voldemort gently took hold of Harry’s chin. “The chance to wed her daughter to a wizard so powerful, he destroyed six hundred years of ancient Malfoy blood-wards in one fell swoop.”

“Somehow, I still think marrying her offspring off to Voldemort Junior takes the cake, for her. The really creepy, really disgusting cake.”

“You underestimate yourself, as always.”

“And you overestimate me, as always.”

“No,” said Voldemort, slowly, studiedly. “I do not.”

As Voldemort was still holding Harry’s chin, he got an intimate view of how Harry went gradually, deeply red, as if his very flesh was being illuminated from within, suffused by the heat of some inner fire. It was mesmerising. “Fine,” mumbled Harry, weakly, and then batted Voldemort’s hands away to study himself in the mirror. “Yikes,” he said, though he said it somewhat admiringly.

“What?” Voldemort frowned, pulling Harry back to work on buttoning his collar. “You look wonderful.”

“I look like a high-class tart,” Harry complained. Voldemort had wrangled him into another of Tabitha’s finely tailored silk shirts—black, this time, the collar a solid band high enough to encircle Harry’s neck completely, with a silver, serpent-shaped button in the front, atop Harry’s Adam’s apple, matching the delicate serpent cufflinks at his wrists. “Like a classy escort in an expensive bondage club, or—no, wait, fancier than that—a concubine! A very kinky emperor’s concubine.” Harry chortled, impressed by his own wit. “I mean, this collar is practically a collar, isn’t it?”

Voldemort was silent in response. He determinedly did not dwell on anything Harry had just said, not only because its sheer crassness would infuriate him into chastising Harry, but because he would end up imagining Harry in some illicit venue, kneeling sweetly for a taller man, head tilted back and kohl-lined eyes half-lidded, rouged-red mouth open and waiting…

Harry’s already breathtaking beauty was not presently embellished by kohl or rouge, thank Merlin, or he would be even more difficult than usual to shield from the avaricious regard of strangers. Voldemort scowled at his own hands where they were buttoning the serpent. If Orion Black had any sense, he would refrain from entertaining inappropriate thoughts of Harry, or would risk being relegated to Mulciber’s category—men doomed to a tortured death as soon as Harry was looking the other way.

Eventually, Voldemort managed, in an attempt to return Harry’s humour with humour, “Bold of you to assume that Walburga Black’s home isn’t as depraved, if not more depraved and more upholstered in studded black leather, than the interiors of those clubs.”

Harry quirked a brow. “You sound like you’ve been to one.”

“Yes. Walburga Black’s home. I believe I made that quite clear. There are dungeons down there more well-equipped with restraints than the cells of Azkaban… though, admittedly, not for enjoyable reasons. Besides,” Voldemort said, “what happened to that condiment?”

Harry blinked. “C-condiment?”

“A spice, if I recall. A posh spice? Was that not what you last compared yourself to?”

Harry startled Voldemort by snickering.

Harry seemed so happy lately, Voldemort thought fondly. When Harry had arrived, he had scarcely sported a smile, but he was freer with his joy, now. It was a sight to behold.

When Harry regained his composure, he hedged, “Um. There’s a lot to unpack in that term, so how about I do it some other day? Because if we don’t leave now, Mrs Black will think we’re late. And since you reckon she’s some type of sadistic dominatrix, I’m not sure I want to piss her off.”

“She will lay neither hand nor whip on you, my dear.”

“Or she’ll lose both?” Harry drifted closer, raising his hands to carefully fasten Voldemort’s own cloak’s clasp. His eyes were uncannily like what they’d been in Voldemort’s imagination, in that fictitious club—half-lidded and glittering with a strange pleasure, giving the impression of darkness despite not being darkened by kohl. “You’ll nail her severed hands to the gates as a warning, sure. But what will you do,” Harry’s fingers trailed down Voldemort’s chest as he stepped away, “with that whip?”

Voldemort again found himself incapable of an answer. Harry was stunning—clad all in black, the fabric of both his shirt and his trousers enchanted to give the subtle, barely-there impression of glimmering, shifting scales. Deadly and beautiful as a Pseudechis. And deadliest, most beautiful of all was Harry’s smile, a thing of cunning and challenge, as though Harry had somehow dared him—

Dared him to do what?

Then Harry shook himself, going utterly crimson, even more so than he had before, with his chin in Voldemort’s grasp. “Er. I don’t—sorry about—” He ran a hand through his hair, mussing it up even further, into a tangle of wild, wet-seeming curls. “I dunno what came over me. Obviously, what you, as my dad, might or might not do with whips is completely irrelevant to me.”

“What did I say about never apologising to me?” Voldemort tutted as he cast a warming charm on Harry. He manfully ignored the spike of heat that had lodged itself in his gut at Harry’s incomprehensible dare, at the sharp, crushed-glass glitter in Harry’s eyes, though those eyes were now downturned, as if ashamed. Unacceptable. “And I did mention corporal punishment on the day we first met, did I not? What I’d do with whips is hardly a mystery, nor irrelevant to you.”

“Hardly, all right,” Harry muttered, before slipping into his own coat, turning up the collar as if to hide himself from Voldemort’s scrutiny. “Off we go to meet the Always Pure, I guess. What a revolting title.”

“You know of ‘Toujours Pur’?”

“Who doesn’t?” Harry reached for the Floo powder above the mantelpiece in his room; the Floo was locked to entry, but not to exits, for Harry was no prisoner here. Voldemort had Floo-called Walburga soon after breakfast, ordering her to leave her own fireplace unlocked to him and his heir this evening. “Let’s raise some fresh hell, shall we? Enough to make Walburga rethink some of her kinks.”

***

“My lord!” Walburga all but swooned in rapture, ushering them eagerly into the foyer of 12 Grimmauld Place. “We are so honoured to have you and your heir.”

She would have neither of them.

Nonetheless, Walburga was beside herself with glee, so much so that Voldemort would not have been astonished had she literally cleaved in two. When she welcomed them into her shadowy, ponderous, unnerving home, she was clad in uncommonly bright colours—a burnt-orange ballgown and a necklace of polished rubies to show off her ancestral wealth—and had her daughters arrayed behind her like a queen’s handmaids, bedecked in equally garish jewels. Bellatrix, in particular, wore a green velvet gown with a suspiciously low-cut neckline and a single large, ostentatious emerald nestled in her generous cleavage, an emerald that matched, exactly, the shade of Harry’s eyes.

Gauche. Uncouth. Voldemort hated her. He’d never hated her before—had even been fond of her for her talent and her brilliance, and, yes, her worship of him as a god. But he had not permitted her to worship Harry, given that Harry was not distanced from her in age and could therefore be subjected to a far more direct seduction. Bellatrix could only ever make eyes at Voldemort, but with Harry, she could draw near. She could touch.

As she was doing now, with a falsely demure hand on Harry’s forearm, under the guise of guiding him into the drawing room. Voldemort grit his teeth against the Crucio building behind them.

Walburga was shameless, parading her daughters before them like the madam of a brothel parading her wares for gentlemen of influence. Then again, such shamelessness would work on most teenage boys... and Harry was a teenage boy.

Voldemort glanced at Harry sideways, and found not a lust-stricken youth but a profoundly uncomfortable boy who was as stiff as a board in all but the most pertinent parts of his anatomy, and who was cooperating with Bellatrix much as a man being led to his execution might cooperate with his guards—grudgingly and with muted, abject horror.

However, with his customary compassion, Harry’s horror melted into concern when he saw Andromeda attempting to vanish into the wallpaper. Oh, Voldemort hated her the most. Narcissa, he could tolerate, given that she had both the dignity and the poise to not throw herself at Harry like Bellatrix, nor attract Harry’s sympathy like Andromeda. Yet she was aesthetically pleasing in her own pale, golden way, like a gilded lily—a serene, self-possessed girl that Harry might find appealing, were they to engage in conversation. Intelligent conversation, as Narcissa was no doubt capable of, despite being the youngest daughter at just thirteen, too young to have been present at the Malfoys’ ball.

On second thought, Voldemort hated her, too. Child though she was.

Still, he was here for a reason, and Voldemort had to put aside his desire to roast Walburga alive above a bonfire fed by the torn-off limbs of her daughters. If he succeeded here today, he and Harry would be able to leave for the future together, their future, where none of these upstarts could follow them.

And where Walburga would, blessedly, be dead.

***

Trays upon trays of elegant canapés were carried into the Blacks’ drawing room by a parade of palpably terrified house-elves, some of them shaking enough to make the platters rattle minutely. Walburga surveyed them with satisfaction, as though having petrified house-elves was the mark of a privileged Pureblood house.

Well. It was.

Unfortunately, it was also costing her Harry’s favour... not that she’d ever had it.

Harry’s mien grew cooler and cooler with each subsequent house-elf, but then, quite suddenly and unnervingly, shifted into a charming smile. Only Narcissa seemed to take note of this change, with narrowed eyes. The rest of her family was oblivious—Bellatrix’s focus was fixed carnivorously on Harry’s throat, on that beautiful collar, Andromeda was looking anywhere but at Harry, and Walburga had the perceptiveness of a side of ham.

“Mrs Black,” said Harry graciously, “your house-elves are exceptionally well-trained.”

She puffed up like a feather duster.

“My father and I…” Harry beseeched her with earnest, innocent eyes. “We’re bachelors, you see, the both of us. So we have but a solitary elf and no-one to train it, as we would if either of us were… married.” He let his eyes dart briefly towards Bellatrix’s chest and then back again. Calling Flopsy ‘it’ instead of ‘her’ was another masterful manoeuvre. “We would certainly—that is, it would—perhaps we could… borrow your house-elves, occasionally?”

Oh, he was Voldemort’s boy, through and through. His lovely, clever boy. Harry knew that Walburga would deny him nothing, and would do him one better; if he asked to borrow her house-elves, she’d give them to him wholly, as gifts.

Which she predictably did.

Walburga tittered. “You can have them all, my young lord! They are yours. Everything we have,” she leered suggestively at her daughters, “is yours.”

“Please,” Harry’s smile grew teeth, “call me Harry.”

Walburga glowed.

And Voldemort fought the compelling urge to drag Harry somewhere nobody could see them, crush him against a wall, and feel what his magic was doing right now. Even from this distance, sitting across from Harry on the throne-like armchair that must typically be reserved for the master of the house, Voldemort could see Harry’s magic shimmer, sly and pleased. As Slytherin as Parseltongue itself.

Bellatrix’s mouth was parted, as though she could sense it, too. Breathe it in. Taste it. Her eyes were wide and lambent as black pools, darting from Harry’s smiling lips to the air around him, to his magical aura.

Idly, Voldemort contemplated destroying her memories, her very mind, so that she could not remember what Harry’s magic felt like. It would be a waste; she was an exceedingly talented witch with a sensitivity to magic that rivalled Voldemort’s. But needs must.

Infuriatingly, Bellatrix had seated herself directly beside Harry on the longest couch, with Andromeda perched uncomfortably on her other side, while Narcissa and Walburga sat on the shorter couch.

Given Narcissa’s slightly raised eyebrow, she was the only Black in the room who had detected the duplicity of Harry’s request. Perhaps she would be the more apt recruit. If she survived the night, that is.

“Why is Orion not present?” Voldemort inevitably asked, and Walburga stiffened, face paling.

“I am afraid he is… unwell, my lord, and hence unable to join us on this splendid evening. He is currently in the Nightingale wing of St Mungo’s, recovering from what seems like an infection of the—”

It was then that Walburga was interrupted, and by the least likely candidate for rude interruptions. Andromeda had finally looked up, a hint of unexpected steel in her eyes. “Mother,” she interjected, her soft voice cloaking its own form of menace, “surely you can assure our lord and his son that our father will be present for their next visit.”

Walburga glowered at Andromeda venomously, and, for once, Andromeda did not flinch away. So there was some spine in her after all.

Voldemort smiled peacefully. It seemed that Walburga had again endeavoured to poison Orion, and that Orion had again stubbornly survived, probably with the assistance of Andromeda, a Herbology prodigy, and Narcissa, the youngest Potions student ever to have been inducted into the Slug Club. Voldemort had read the short report on the Blacks that Morgane had owled him earlier today; staying abreast of his followers’ family circumstances was essential to being aware of what was actually happening behind the scenes in the Pureblood dynasties he sought to rule. If Voldemort did not know their weaknesses, he could not effectively threaten them, blackmail them, bribe them, or otherwise bend them to his will.

“I agree.” Voldemort swirled his wineglass. “It would be pleasant to converse with Orion. He is a valuable member of our organisation, and, if he passed, it would be a terrible loss for us all. Most of all for you, Walburga.” The loss of her head. Voldemort’s tone made it self-evident.

What colour had remained in Walburga’s face drained away, leaving her a chalk-like husk. “Yes,” she said faintly, obediently, despite her noxious hatred for her husband, whom she had suffered to have in her body for the sole purpose of birthing successors. “It shall be as you command, my lord.”

“Where are your sons?” Voldemort queried, because Harry was fidgeting while peeking impatiently at the grand staircase beyond the door, presumably thinking of how to meet up with his accursed Sirius.

“Oh!” Walburga brightened again. “They’re far too young to mind their manners in such illustrious company, my lord, with one being only an infant and the other a toddler. A house-elf is supervising them upstairs…”

Harry sprang up at once. “I’d love to meet them!” he exclaimed. “I mean, uh, babies. Good, Pureblood babies. Er. Are gratifying. To see?”

Voldemort set his wineglass down and sighed. Leave it to Harry to go from the epitome of Slytherin wiliness to woefully transparent Gryffindor bumbling. Not that Walburga could see through it, as she had no notion that her eldest son would ultimately become Harry’s beloved godfather.

Narcissa, whose eyebrow was permanently raised at this point, nonetheless softened her icy, formal demeanour at Harry’s apparent fondness for children. Bellatrix bounded up as well, all seductiveness forgotten, her typically fevered, manic eyes lit with a fierce affection for her brothers. It was surprising, that she loved them. Voldemort had not considered Bellatrix capable of the more tender emotions.

Andromeda stood deliberately, with a determined kind of grace that transformed her plainness into something dignified, and made her way over to Harry to be his escort this time. After taking his arm, she said quietly but significantly, “They might only be babies, Lord Gaunt, but they are subjected to the same rigorous discipline that all proper Pureblood heirs are.”

Was Andromeda trying to signal to Harry that Sirius and Regulus were being abused? Not that it took a great deal of insight to infer that a person of Walburga’s disposition would be an abusive parent.

Walburga had gone a furious shade of puce. She barrelled over Andromeda, scoffing, “Naturally. Gentlemanly manners are only learned through discipline,” she glared daggers at Andromeda, undoubtedly planning her rebellious daughter’s punishment already, although Andromeda did not quail, “and not through a dissolute fulfilment of every childish whim.” She extended an arm proudly towards the stairs leading upwards, into the cavernous bowels of the manor. “Come, my young lord.” She dipped her chin in a deferential semi-curtsey. “I am deeply honoured that you already wish to meet my sons, your future followers.”

“They are indeed our future,” Harry said brightly, though his hands were white-knuckled fists, including the hand whose arm Andromeda gingerly held.

Normally, Voldemort would have been fantasising about cutting off the limb that Andromeda had dared to place on Harry, but he could intuit that her uncharacteristic decision to assert herself as Harry’s escort for this portion of the house tour was not a ploy for Harry’s attention, but rather a means of placing herself within a close enough distance to subtly communicate with him about her younger brothers’ welfare.

Bellatrix and Narcissa had shifted forward on either side of the pair that Andromeda and Harry made, so that the girls resembled some sort of militant sisterly squad falling into a much-accustomed formation against a common enemy. Walburga stood alone in front of them, visibly abandoned by her own progeny before her lord, and her angry, humiliated flush darkened from puce to mauve.

Curious. Despite being a seemingly undivided family traditionally renowned for its unity, the Blacks had as many factions as Slytherin itself, and it appeared that Walburga was facing a bit of a mutiny. A sign of poor leadership.

“Lead the way, Andromeda,” Voldemort granted magnanimously, addressing the daughter instead of the mother and mistress of the household, and was rewarded both by Walburga’s horrified dismay at being slighted by her lord, and by the vicious, vengeful flash in Harry’s green eyes.

Voldemort smirked. If any harm had been done to Sirius, maybe Voldemort would see a follower of his beheaded by Harry tonight.

Dreams could, perhaps, come true.
